========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess the main reason I’m here is that I keep not finishing things all the way through, even when I really mean to. Like at work, I’ll start the sanitation steps, or I’ll be doing batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork, and I know exactly that those things matter — it’s not like I think they’re optional or I don’t care. I know my job depends on them being done correctly.

But somehow I’ll get most of the way through and then leave out the last parts. It’s like I start the process and I’m moving through it, and then something just… drops off. And then later I realize, or someone points it out, that I didn’t finish the final piece or missed the last section, and it’s really stressful because these aren’t little things I can just brush off. I’m coming in because it keeps happening, and I don’t want to keep messing up things that I actually know are important.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it’s definitely not new. I mean, the work stuff is what made it feel urgent now, because the consequences are bigger, but when I think back, it’s been there a long time.

My mom actually still has some of my old elementary school report cards — I think around fourth grade — and they say things like I was bright, I understood the material, but I needed constant reminders to finish my classwork or to turn in homework. And I remember that pretty clearly too. It wasn’t like I didn’t understand the lessons. I’d get what the teacher was explaining, but then I’d miss parts of the instructions or skip chunks of what I was supposed to do unless the teacher was kind of nearby and checking in on me.

So yeah, I guess it’s been a pattern since I was a kid. It just looked like schoolwork then, and now it’s showing up in job tasks.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes, that happens a lot. It’s not even always like one dramatic distraction, like somebody yelling or something. Sometimes I come in for a shift really trying to be locked in, like I’ll tell myself, okay, pay attention to each step, don’t miss anything, stay on the labels, stay on the weights, follow the process.

But after I’ve been doing it for a while, the cutting and weighing and checking labels kind of all start to run together. Like everything becomes this blur of repeated steps, and then I’ll realize I’m just sort of staring at the same tray or the same log sheet and not actually taking in what I’m supposed to do next. I’m looking at it, but I’m not processing it, if that makes sense.

And then something around me can pull me off too — people moving nearby, noises from the equipment, someone asking a question — but sometimes it’s not even that obvious. My attention just drifts, and then I sort of come back and think, wait, where was I in the process? Did I already check that label, or was I about to check it? That part is really frustrating because I’m trying hard, but the focus doesn’t stay steady.

And I remember that pretty clearly too. It wasn’t like I didn’t understand the lessons. I’d get what the teacher was explaining, but then I’d miss parts of the instructions or skip chunks of what I was supposed to do unless the teacher was kind of nearby and checking in on me.\n\nSo yeah, I guess it’s been a pattern since I was a kid. It just looked like schoolwork then, and now it’s showing up in job tasks.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble finishing tasks, have you noticed things like feeling restless, having a hard time sitting still, fidgeting, or acting impulsively—like interrupting others or blurting things out?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. At least not in the way you’re describing it. I’m not usually someone who feels like I have to get up and move around all the time, or like I can’t physically stay seated. I mean, I might shift around if I’m uncomfortable or nervous, but I don’t think people would describe me as restless.\n\nAnd with interrupting or blurting things out… I don’t think that’s a big issue for me either. I’m actually more likely to hang back or overthink what I’m going to say than just jump in. So, no, I haven’t really noticed that kind of impulsive thing being a pattern. It’s not even always like one dramatic distraction, like somebody yelling or something. Sometimes I come in for a shift really trying to be locked in, like I’ll tell myself, okay, pay attention to each step, don’t miss anything, stay on the labels, stay on the weights, follow the process.\n\nBut after I’ve been doing it for a while, the cutting and weighing and checking labels kind of all start to run together. Like everything becomes this blur of repeated steps, and then I’ll realize I’m just sort of staring at the same tray or the same log sheet and not actually taking in what I’m supposed to do next. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely. I lose stuff constantly, especially at work, and it’s usually things I need right then, which makes it more embarrassing and stressful.

Like on the floor, I’ll set down my cut glove, or my marker, or my badge, or my safety glasses, and then two minutes later I have no idea where they are. And the worst part is I’ll swear I literally just had it in my hand. Like I can picture myself holding the marker or having the glove with me, but then it’s gone, and I end up retracing my steps around the same area for ten minutes trying to figure out where I put it.

It’s not even like I’m leaving things in random places on purpose. It’s more like I put something down for one second while I’m doing another step, and my brain just doesn’t register where it went. Then I’m checking the same cart, the same table, my pockets, back to the cart again, and I start feeling really stupid because it looks like I’m just being careless. But it happens a lot.

Like everything becomes this blur of repeated steps, and then I’ll realize I’m just sort of staring at the same tray or the same log sheet and not actually taking in what I’m supposed to do next. I’m looking at it, but I’m not processing it, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then something around me can pull me off too — people moving nearby, noises from the equipment, someone asking a question — but sometimes it’s not even that obvious. My attention just drifts, and then I sort of come back and think, wait, where was I in the process? Did I already check that label, or was I about to check it? That part is really frustrating because I’m trying hard, but the focus doesn’t stay steady.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself losing track of things you need, like keys, paperwork, or your phone, or struggling to keep your workspace and daily tasks organized?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I lose stuff constantly, especially at work, and it’s usually things I need right then, which makes it more embarrassing and stressful.\n\nLike on the floor, I’ll set down my cut glove, or my marker, or my badge, or my safety glasses, and then two minutes later I have no idea where they are. And the worst part is I’ll swear I literally just had it in my hand. Like I can picture myself holding the marker or having the glove with me, but then it’s gone, and I end up retracing my steps around the same area for ten minutes trying to figure out where I put it.\n\nIt’s not even like I’m leaving things in random places on purpose. It’s more like I put something down for one second while I’m doing another step, and my brain just doesn’t register where it went. Then I’m checking the same cart, the same table, my pockets, back to the cart again, and I start feeling really stupid because it looks like I’m just being careless. But it happens a lot.\n\n[Last Question]\nEven if you don't move around much physically, do you ever notice a sense of inner restlessness or your mind racing, like you can't relax internally?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / butcher_or_meat_poultry_or_fish_processing_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I don’t think I feel restless in the sense of, like, I need to jump out of my skin or I can’t sit still. Internally… maybe sometimes I feel kind of wound up if I’m stressed, but I don’t know if I’d call it my mind racing exactly.

Like, there are times when I’m trying to relax and I’m still thinking about things, but I’m not sure that’s more than normal. I wouldn’t say I have this constant internal motor feeling or anything like that. It’s more… I don’t know, I can get mentally stuck or preoccupied, but I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern of feeling unable to relax inside.

Like at work, I’ll start the sanitation steps, or I’ll be doing batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork, and I know exactly that those things matter — it’s not like I think they’re optional or I don’t care. I know my job depends on them being done correctly.\n\nBut somehow I’ll get most of the way through and then leave out the last parts. It’s like I start the process and I’m moving through it, and then something just… drops off. And then later I realize, or someone points it out, that I didn’t finish the final piece or missed the last section, and it’s really stressful because these aren’t little things I can just brush off. I’m coming in because it keeps happening, and I don’t want to keep messing up things that I actually know are important.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this difficulty finishing tasks has been a pattern for you since childhood, or is this something that started more recently in your life?\n\nPatient: No, it’s definitely not new. I mean, the work stuff is what made it feel urgent now, because the consequences are bigger, but when I think back, it’s been there a long time.\n\nMy mom actually still has some of my old elementary school report cards — I think around fourth grade — and they say things like I was bright, I understood the material, but I needed constant reminders to finish my classwork or to turn in homework. And I remember that pretty clearly too. It wasn’t like I didn’t understand the lessons. I’d get what the teacher was explaining, but then I’d miss parts of the instructions or skip chunks of what I was supposed to do unless the teacher was kind of nearby and checking in on me.\n\nSo yeah, I guess it’s been a pattern since I was a kid. It just looked like schoolwork then, and now it’s showing up in job tasks.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble finishing tasks, have you noticed things like feeling restless, having a hard time sitting still, fidgeting, or acting impulsively—like interrupting others or blurting things out?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. And the worst part is I’ll swear I literally just had it in my hand. Like I can picture myself holding the marker or having the glove with me, but then it’s gone, and I end up retracing my steps around the same area for ten minutes trying to figure out where I put it.\n\nIt’s not even like I’m leaving things in random places on purpose. It’s more like I put something down for one second while I’m doing another step, and my brain just doesn’t register where it went. Then I’m checking the same cart, the same table, my pockets, back to the cart again, and I start feeling really stupid because it looks like I’m just being careless. But it happens a lot.\n\nDoctor: Even if you don't move around much physically, do you ever notice a sense of inner restlessness or your mind racing, like you can't relax internally?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I don’t think I feel restless in the sense of, like, I need to jump out of my skin or I can’t sit still. Internally… maybe sometimes I feel kind of wound up if I’m stressed, but I don’t know if I’d call it my mind racing exactly.\n\nLike, there are times when I’m trying to relax and I’m still thinking about things, but I’m not sure that’s more than normal. I wouldn’t say I have this constant internal motor feeling or anything like that. It’s more… I don’t know, I can get mentally stuck or preoccupied, but I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern of feeling unable to relax inside.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself talking more than others feel is appropriate, or having trouble waiting your turn in conversations or lines?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / butcher_or_meat_poultry_or_fish_processing_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I don’t think so, no. I mean, I can talk a lot if I’m nervous or if I’m trying to explain something fully — like I know I’m doing that right now, probably — but I don’t think people usually tell me I’m talking too much or that I’m interrupting them all the time.

Waiting in lines… I mean, I don’t love it, but I don’t think I have a major problem with it. I’m usually just kind of standing there, maybe annoyed like anyone would be, but not cutting people off or unable to wait. And in conversations, I try to wait my turn. Sometimes I might over-explain because I want to make sure I’m being understood, but I wouldn’t say I often talk more than is appropriate or can’t let other people speak.

Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future. Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. I’m looking at it, but I’m not processing it, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then something around me can pull me off too — people moving nearby, noises from the equipment, someone asking a question — but sometimes it’s not even that obvious. My attention just drifts, and then I sort of come back and think, wait, where was I in the process? Did I already check that label, or was I about to check it? That part is really frustrating because I’m trying hard, but the focus doesn’t stay steady.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }

I lose stuff constantly, especially at work, and it’s usually things I need right then, which makes it more embarrassing and stressful.\n\nLike on the floor, I’ll set down my cut glove, or my marker, or my badge, or my safety glasses, and then two minutes later I have no idea where they are. And the worst part is I’ll swear I literally just had it in my hand. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.
